Knudsen boundary layer equations for full ranges of cutoff collision kernels: Maxwell reflection boundary with all accommodation coefficients in [0,1] Thanks: ∗ Corresponding author August 24, 2026
Ning Jiang, Yi-Long Luo ∗, Yulong Wu

Abstract
. In this paper, we prove the existence and uniqueness of the Knudsen layer equation imposed on Maxwell reflection boundary condition with full ranges of cutoff collision kernels and accommodation coefficients (i.e., − 3 < γ ≤ 1 -3<\gamma\leq 1 and 0 ≤ α ∗ ≤ 1 0\leq\alpha_{*}\leq 1 , respectively) in the L x , v ∞ L^{\infty}_{x,v} framework. Moreover, the solution enjoys the exponential decay exp ⁡ { − c ​ x 2 3 − γ − c ​ | v | 2 } \mathrm{exp}\{-cx^{\frac{2}{3-\gamma}}-c|v|^{2}\} for some c > 0 c>0 . In order to study the general angular cutoff collision kernel − 3 < γ ≤ 1 -3<\gamma\leq 1 , we should introduce a ( x , v ) (x,v) -mixed weight σ \sigma . The biggest difficulty in this paper is the nondissipative boundary condition, hence, the boundary temperature and velocity ( T w , u w ) (T_{w},u_{w}) on { x = 0 } \{x=0\} and ( T , 𝔲 ) (T,\mathfrak{u}) on { x = + ∞ } \{x=+\infty\} do not guarantee the nonnegativity of the L 2 L^{2} boundary energy. We also do not assume that ( T w , u w ) (T_{w},u_{w}) and ( T , 𝔲 ) (T,\mathfrak{u}) are very closed to each other. We first derive the Nondissipative boundary lemma to pull the boundary energy to the interior weighted L 2 L^{2} norms with higher power of x x -polynomial weights. Then a so-called spatial-velocity indices iteration approach is developed to shift the higher power x x -polynomial weights to | v | |v| -polynomial weights. Finally, we construct an interleaved iteration process such that the boundary energy is successfully dominated. Keywords. Knudsen layer equation, Maxwell reflection boundary, exponential decay, nondissipative boundary MSC2020. 35Q20, 76P05, 35M32, 35B45, 35A01, 35A02



1.
Introduction and main results

1.1.
The description of the problem

When studying the hydrodynamic limits of the scaled Boltzmann equation in the domain with boundary, there is an essential kinetic boundary layer equation, called the Knudsen layer equation, will be generated, see
[
1
,
11
,
16
,
17
,
21
,
22
]
for instance. In this paper, we consider the Knudsen layer equation over
(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
with the Maxwell boundary condition at
x=0
:

(KL)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &v_{3}\partial_{x}f+\mathcal{L}f=S\,,\\
&f(0,v)|_{v_{3}>0}=(1-\alpha_{*})f(0,R_{0}v)+\alpha_{*}\mathcal{D}_{w}f(0,v)+f_{b}(v)\,,\ \lim_{x\to+\infty}f(x,v)=0\,,\end{aligned}\right.

where
S=S(x,v)\in\mathrm{Null}^{\perp}(\mathcal{L})
,
f_{b}(v)=0
if
v_{3}<0
. At
x=0
, we define an external normal vector
n(0)=(0,0,-1)
.
R_{0}v=v-2(n(0)\cdot v)n(0)=(v_{1},v_{2},-v_{3})
represents the specular reflection at
x=0
.
f(0,R_{0}v)
then characterizes the specular reflection effect at the boundary
x=0
. The operator
\mathcal{D}_{w}f(0,v)
is the diffusive reflection operator, which is defined by

(1.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{w}f(0,v)=\tfrac{M_{w}(v)}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}(v)}}\int_{v_{3}^{\prime}<0}(-v_{3}^{\prime})f(0,v^{\prime})\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}(v^{\prime})}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\,.

Here
M_{w}(v)
is the Maxwellian of the boundary with the expression

\displaystyle M_{w}(v)=\sqrt{\tfrac{2\pi}{T_{w}}}\tfrac{1}{(2\pi T_{w})^{\frac{3}{2}}}\mathrm{exp}\big[-\tfrac{|v-u_{w}|^{2}}{2T_{w}}\big]\,,

where
T_{w}>0
is the temperature of the boundary, and
u_{w}=(u_{w,1},u_{w,2},u_{w,3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
u_{w,3}=0
is the velocity of the boundary. Remark that
M_{w}(v)
satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{v_{3}>0}v_{3}M_{w}(v)\mathrm{d}v=1\,,

which means that the particles absorbed by the boundary will be completely released by the way of Gaussian distribution. The constant
\alpha_{*}\in[0,1]
is the accommodation coefficient weighting the specular reflection effect and the diffusive reflection effect.

The symbol
\mathcal{L}
is the linearized Boltzmann collision operator
\mathcal{B}(F,F)
around the Maxwellian

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}(v)=\tfrac{\rho}{(2\pi T)^{3/2}}\mathrm{exp}\big[-\tfrac{|v-\mathfrak{u}|^{2}}{2T}\big]\,,

where
\rho,T>0
and
\mathfrak{u}=(\mathfrak{u}_{1},\mathfrak{u}_{2},\mathfrak{u}_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
\mathfrak{u}_{3}=0
are all constant. We remark that
T>0
and
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
exactly are the temperature and velocity of the far-field boundary of the Knudsen layer equation. It can be intuitively observed in the nonlinear problem (
KL-NL
) later.

Furthermore, the Boltzmann collision operator
\mathcal{B}(F,F)
is defined by

(1.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(F,F)=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}(F^{\prime}F_{*}^{\prime}-FF_{*})b(\omega,v_{*}-v)\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}v_{*}\,.

Here
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
is a unit vector,
\mathrm{d}\omega
is the rotationally invariant surface integral on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, while
F_{*}^{\prime}
,
F^{\prime}
,
F_{*}
and
F
are the number density
F(\cdot)
evaluated at the velocities
v_{*}^{\prime}
,
v^{\prime}
,
v_{*}
and
v
respectively, i.e.,

\displaystyle F_{*}^{\prime}=F(v_{*}^{\prime})\,,\ F^{\prime}=F(v^{\prime})\,,\ F_{*}=F(v_{*})\,,\ F=F(v)\,.

Here
(v_{*}^{\prime},v^{\prime})
are the velocities after an elastic binary collision between two molecules with velocities
(v_{*},v)
before the collision, or vice versa. Since both momentum and energy are conserved during the elastic collision,
v_{*}^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}
can be expressed in terms of
v_{*}
and
v
as

(1.3)

\displaystyle v_{*}^{\prime}=v_{*}-[(v_{*}-v)\cdot\omega]\omega\,,\ v^{\prime}=v+[(v_{*}-v)\cdot\omega]\omega\,,

where the unit vector
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
is parallel to the deflections
v_{*}^{\prime}-v_{*}
and
v^{\prime}-v
, and is therefore perpendicular to the plane of reflection. In the collision term
\mathcal{B}(F,F)
,
F_{*}^{\prime}F^{\prime}
is the gain term, while
F_{*}F
is called the loss term.

By Grad’s work
[
12
]
, the collision kernel
b(\omega,v_{*}-v)
has the factored form

(1.4)

\displaystyle b(\omega,v_{*}-v)=\tilde{b}(\theta)|v_{*}-v|^{\gamma}\,,\ \cos\theta=\tfrac{(v_{*}-v)\cdot\omega}{|v_{*}-v|}\,,\ -3<\gamma\leq 1\,,

where
\tilde{b}(\theta)
satisfies the small deflection cutoff condition

(1.5)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\tilde{b}(\theta)\mathrm{d}\omega=1\,,\ 0\leq\tilde{b}(\theta)\leq\tilde{b}_{0}|\cos\theta|

for some constant
\tilde{b}_{0}>0
. The cases
-3<\gamma<0
and
0\leq\gamma\leq 1
are respectively referred to as the “soft” and “hard” potential cases. In particular,
\gamma=0
is the Maxwell potential case, and
\gamma=1
is the hard sphere case, in which
\tilde{b}(\theta)=\tilde{b}_{0}|\cos\theta|
.

Considering the perturbation
F=\mathfrak{M}+\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}f
around
\mathfrak{M}
, the linearized Boltzmann collision operator
\mathcal{L}
is defined by

(1.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}f=-\mathfrak{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(v)\big[\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{M},\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}f)+\mathcal{B}(\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}f,\mathfrak{M})\big]=\nu(v)f(v)-Kf(v)\,,

where the collision frequency
\nu(v)
is defined by

(1.7)

\displaystyle\nu(v)=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathfrak{M}(v_{*})b(\omega,v_{*}-v)\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}v_{*}\overset{\eqref{Cutoff}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|v_{*}-v|^{\gamma}\mathfrak{M}(v_{*})\mathrm{d}v_{*}\,,

and the operator
Kf(v)
can be decomposed into two parts:

(1.8)

\displaystyle Kf(v)=-K_{1}f(v)+K_{2}f(v)\,.

Here the loss term
K_{1}f(v)
is

(1.9)

\displaystyle K_{1}f(v)=\mathfrak{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}(v)\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}f(v_{*})\mathfrak{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}(v_{*})b(\omega,v_{*}-v)\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}v_{*}\,,

and the gain term
K_{2}f(v)
is

(1.10)

\displaystyle K_{2}f(v)=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}(v)\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\big[\mathfrak{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(v^{\prime})f(v^{\prime})+\mathfrak{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(v_{*}^{\prime})f(v_{*}^{\prime})\big]\mathfrak{M}(v_{*})b(\omega,v_{*}-v)\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}v_{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}\big[\mathfrak{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}(v^{\prime}_{*})f(v^{\prime})+\mathfrak{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}(v^{\prime})f(v_{*}^{\prime})\big]\mathfrak{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}(v_{*})b(\omega,v_{*}-v)\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}v_{*}\,,

where the last equality is derived from the collisional invariant
\mathfrak{M}(v)\mathfrak{M}(v_{*})=\mathfrak{M}(v^{\prime})\mathfrak{M}(v_{*}^{\prime})
. Remark that the collision frequency is invariant under the specular reflection operator, i.e.,

(1.11)

\displaystyle\nu(R_{0}v)=\nu(v)\,.

The null space
\mathrm{Null}(\mathcal{L})
of the operator
\mathcal{L}
is spanned by the basis

(1.12)

\displaystyle\psi_{0}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\rho}}\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}\,,\ \psi_{i}=\tfrac{v_{i}-\mathfrak{u}_{i}}{\sqrt{\rho T}}\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}\,(i=1,2,3)\,,\ \psi_{4}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{6\rho}}\left(\tfrac{|v-\mathfrak{u}|^{2}}{T}-3\right)\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}\,.

The basis is orthonormal in the Hilbert space
L^{2}:=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Namely,
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\psi_{i}\psi_{j}\mathrm{d}v=\delta_{ij}
for
i,j=0,1,2,3,4
. Let
\mathrm{Null}^{\perp}(\mathcal{L})
be the orthogonal space of
\mathrm{Null}(\mathcal{L})
in
L^{2}
, namely,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Null}(\mathcal{L})\oplus\mathrm{Null}^{\perp}(\mathcal{L})=L^{2}\,.

We define the projection
\mathcal{P}:L^{2}\to\mathrm{Null}(\mathcal{L})
by

(1.13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}f=\sum_{i=0}^{4}a_{i}(f)\psi_{i}\,,\ a_{i}(f)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f\psi_{i}\mathrm{d}v\,,i=0,1,2,3,4\,.

Based on the projection
\mathcal{P}
, over the space
\mathrm{Null}(\mathcal{L})
, we introduce the operator

(1.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{P}v_{3}\mathcal{P}\,,

which is the 5-dimensional linear bounded self adjoint operator. It is easy to find that
\mathcal{A}
possesses the eigenvalues

(1.15)

\lambda_{i}=0\ (i=0,1,2)\,,\ \lambda_{3}=\sqrt{\tfrac{5}{3}T}\,,\lambda_{4}=-\sqrt{\tfrac{5}{3}T}\,.

The corresponding unit eigenvectors are

(1.16)

\displaystyle\psi_{0}^{*}(v)=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\rho}}(\tfrac{|v-\mathfrak{u}|^{2}}{T}-5)\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}\,,\ \psi_{i}^{*}(v)=\tfrac{v_{i}-\mathfrak{u}_{i}}{\sqrt{\rho T}}\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}(i=1,2)\,,

\displaystyle\psi_{3}^{*}(v)=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\rho}}(\tfrac{|v-\mathfrak{u}|^{2}}{T}+\sqrt{15}\tfrac{v_{3}}{\sqrt{T}})\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}\,,\ \psi_{4}^{*}(v)=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\rho}}(\tfrac{|v-\mathfrak{u}|^{2}}{T}-\sqrt{15}\tfrac{v_{3}}{\sqrt{T}})\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}\,,

which forms an orthonormal basis of the null space
\mathrm{Null}(\mathcal{L})
. Then one can define the projection
\mathcal{P}^{+}
from
\mathrm{Null}(\mathcal{L})
to the subspace associated with positive eigenvalues of
\mathcal{A}
by

(1.17)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{+}f=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\psi_{3}^{*}f\mathrm{d}v\psi_{3}^{*}\,.

We now introduce the Burnett functions
\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
and
\mathbb{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
with the entries

(1.18)

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{ij}=\big\{\tfrac{(v_{i}-\mathfrak{u}_{i})(v_{j}-\mathfrak{u}_{j})}{T}-\delta_{ij}\tfrac{|v-\mathfrak{u}|^{2}}{3T}\big\}\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}\ (1\leq i,j\leq 3)\,,

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{i}=\tfrac{v_{i}-\mathfrak{u}_{i}}{2\sqrt{T}}(\tfrac{|v-\mathfrak{u}|^{2}}{T}-5)\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}\ (1\leq i\leq 3)\,.

The entries above belonging to
\mathrm{Null}^{\perp}(\mathcal{L})
are all orthogonal each other. We also define

(1.19)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{ij}=\mathcal{L}^{-1}\mathbb{A}_{ij}\ (1\leq i,j\leq 3)\,,\ \widehat{\mathbb{B}}_{i}=\mathcal{L}^{-1}\mathbb{B}_{i}\ (1\leq i\leq 3)\,,

where the notation
\mathcal{L}^{-1}
is the inverse of
\mathcal{L}
restricted on the orthogonal space
\mathrm{Null}^{\perp}(\mathcal{L})
, whose decay properties can be found in
[
18
]
. As shown in Golse’s work
[
7
]
, we introduce the projection
\mathcal{P}^{0}
by

(1.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{0}f=\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}v_{3}\widehat{\mathbb{B}}_{3}f\mathrm{d}v}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\widehat{\mathbb{B}}_{3}\mathbb{B}_{3}\mathrm{d}v}\psi_{0}^{*}+\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}v_{3}\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{13}f\mathrm{d}v}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{13}\mathbb{A}_{13}\mathrm{d}v}\psi_{1}^{*}+\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}v_{3}\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{23}f\mathrm{d}v}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{23}\mathbb{A}_{23}\mathrm{d}v}\psi_{2}^{*}\,,

which is actually a map from
L^{2}
to the subspace of
\mathrm{Null}(\mathcal{L})
associated with the zero eigenvalues. Moreover, Golse
[
7
]
also defined the operator
\mathbb{P}
from
\mathrm{Null}^{\perp}(\mathcal{L})
to
\mathrm{Span}\{\mathbb{A}_{12},\mathbb{A}_{13},\mathbb{B}_{3}\}
by

(1.21)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}f=\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\widehat{\mathbb{B}}_{3}f\mathrm{d}v}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\widehat{\mathbb{B}}_{3}\mathbb{B}_{3}\mathrm{d}v}\mathbb{B}_{3}+\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{13}f\mathrm{d}v}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{13}\mathbb{A}_{13}\mathrm{d}v}\mathbb{A}_{13}+\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{23}f\mathrm{d}v}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{23}\mathbb{A}_{23}\mathrm{d}v}\mathbb{A}_{23}\,.

By Lemma 3 of
[
7
]
, the operators
\mathcal{P}^{0}
and
\mathbb{P}
enjoy the following properties:

•

\mathbb{P}^{2}=\mathbb{P}
,
(\mathcal{P}^{0})^{2}=\mathcal{P}^{0}
;

•

\mathrm{Im}\mathbb{P}=\mathrm{Span}\{\mathbb{A}_{13},\mathbb{A}_{23},\mathbb{B}_{3}\}\subset\mathrm{Null}^{\perp}(\mathcal{L})
,
\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{P}^{0}=\mathrm{Span}\{\psi_{0}^{*},\psi_{1}^{*},\psi_{2}^{*}\}\subset\mathrm{Null}(\mathcal{L})
;

•

\mathbb{P}(v_{3}f)=v_{3}\mathcal{P}^{0}f
;

•

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}f)=0
if
v_{3}f\perp\mathrm{Null}(\mathcal{L})
;

•

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f\mathcal{P}^{0}f\mathrm{d}v\geq 0
, and the map
f\mapsto(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f\mathcal{P}^{0}f\mathrm{d}v)^{\frac{1}{2}}
defines a norm on
\mathrm{Span}\{\psi_{0}^{*},\psi_{1}^{*},\psi_{2}^{*}\}
.

1.2.
Overdetermined far-field condition
\lim_{x\to+\infty}f(x,v)=0

As inspired in Theorem 3.3 of
[
2
]
, the function
f(x,v)
solving the problem

(1.22)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &v_{3}\partial_{x}f+\mathcal{L}f=S\,,\ x>0\,,\\
&f(0,v)|_{v_{3}>0}=(1-\alpha_{*})f(0,R_{0}v)+\alpha_{*}\mathcal{D}_{w}f(0,v)+f_{b}(v)\end{aligned}\right.

will enjoy the asymptotic behavior

(1.23)

\displaystyle\lim_{x\to+\infty}f(x,v)=a_{\infty}\psi_{0}(v)+b_{1\infty}\psi_{1}(v)+b_{2\infty}\psi_{2}(v)+b_{3\infty}\psi_{3}(v)+c_{\infty}\psi_{4}(v)

for some constants
a_{\infty}
,
b_{1\infty}
,
b_{2\infty}
,
b_{3\infty}
and
c_{\infty}
, where the functions
\psi_{i}(v)
are given in (
1.12
). In other words, the far-field condition
\lim_{x\to+\infty}f(x,v)=0
in (
KL
) is overdetermined for general source terms
S\in\mathrm{Null}^{\perp}(\mathcal{L})
and
f_{b}
.

However, the far-field condition
\lim_{x\to+\infty}f(x,v)=0
is necessary in proving the hydrodynamic limits (including the compressible/incompressible limits) of the Boltzmann equation with Maxwell reflection boundary condition. In order to overcome the overdetermination of zero far-field condition, some further assumptions on the source terms
S\in\mathrm{Null}^{\perp}(\mathcal{L})
and
f_{b}
are required. Now we introduce a so-called
vanishing sources set

\mathbb{VSS}_{\alpha_{*}}
defined by

(1.24)

\displaystyle\mathbb{VSS}_{\alpha_{*}}=\big\{(S,f_{b});S(x,v)\in\mathrm{Null}^{\perp}(\mathcal{L})\textrm{ and }f_{b}(v)\textrm{ in \eqref{KL-d} such that }\lim_{x\to+\infty}f(x,v)=0\big\}\,.

As shown in
[
8
]
, one knows that for
\alpha_{*}=0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{VSS}_{0}=\Big\{(S,f_{b});S(x,v)\in\mathrm{Null}^{\perp}(\mathcal{L})\,,\ \int_{v_{3}>0}v_{3}\psi_{i}(v)f_{b}(v)\mathrm{d}v=0\,,\ i=0,1,2,4\Big\}\neq\varnothing\,.

For the case
0<\alpha_{*}\leq 1
, by employing the isotropic properties of
\mathcal{L}
(see Appendix B of
[
21
]
and Appendix A.2 of
[
22
]
), one can find some special expressions of
S(x,v)
and
f_{b}(v)
belonging to the so-called vanishing sources set
\mathbb{VSS}_{\alpha_{*}}
. For example, special representations of
S
and
f_{b}
can be found in Problems (106)-(109), Section 5.2 of
[
1
]
, and Problems (4.16)-(4.17), Section 4 of
[
13
]
. Therefore, by summarizing above arguments, we know that for
0\leq\alpha_{*}\leq 1
,

(1.25)

\displaystyle\mathbb{VSS}_{\alpha_{*}}\neq\varnothing\,.

Lately, the structure of the vanishing sources set
\mathbb{VSS}_{\alpha_{*}}
with codimension 4 will be clearly characterized. From now on, we will assume
(S,f_{b})\in\mathbb{VSS}_{\alpha_{*}}
such that the far-field condition
\lim_{x\to+\infty}f(x,v)=0
holds.

1.3.
Toolbox

In this subsection, we will collect all notations, functions spaces and energy functionals that will be utilized in whole paper.

1.3.1.
Notations

We employ the symbol
A\lesssim B
meaning that
A\leq CB
for some harmless constant
C>0
. Moreover,
A\thicksim B
denotes by
C_{1}B\leq A\leq C_{2}B
for some harmless constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
. As inspired in
[
3
]
, we introduce the following
(x,v)
-mixed weight

(1.26)

\displaystyle\sigma(x,v)=5(\delta x+l)^{\frac{2}{3-\gamma}}\Big[1-\Upsilon\Big(\tfrac{\delta x+l}{(1+|v-\mathfrak{u}|)^{3-\gamma}}\Big)\Big]+\Big(\tfrac{\delta x+l}{(1+|v-\mathfrak{u}|)^{1-\gamma}}+3|v-\mathfrak{u}|^{2}\Big)\Upsilon\Big(\tfrac{\delta x+l}{(1+|v-\mathfrak{u}|)^{3-\gamma}}\Big)

for small
\delta>0
and large
l>0
to be determined, where the cutoff function
\Upsilon(\cdot)
is defined in (
1.64
). This weight can also be employed in the works
[
26
,
27
,
28
]
. For any small
\hbar>0
to be determined, denote by

(1.27)

\displaystyle f_{\sigma}:=e^{\hbar\sigma}f\ (\forall f=f(x,v))\,.

We also introduce a weighted function

(1.28)

\displaystyle w_{\beta,\vartheta}(v)=(1+|v|)^{\beta}e^{\vartheta|v-\mathfrak{u}|^{2}}

for the constants
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
and
\vartheta\geq 0
. Then, for
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote a weight function

(1.29)

z_{\alpha}(v)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}|v_{3}|^{\alpha}\,,&|v_{3}|<1\,,\\
1\,,&|v_{3}|\geq 1\,.\\
\end{array}\right.

We remark that the weights
\sigma(x,v)
and
w_{\beta,\vartheta}(v)
satisfy

(1.30)

\displaystyle\sigma(x,R_{U}v)=\sigma(x,v)\,,\ w_{\beta,\vartheta}(R_{U}v)=w_{\beta,\vartheta}(v)\,,\ z_{\alpha}(R_{U}v)=z_{\alpha}(v)\textrm{ for }U=0,A\,,

due to
\mathfrak{u}_{3}=0
. Here
R_{U}v=v-2[n(U)\cdot v]n(U)
,
n(A)=(0,0,1)
. We now define the constant
\beta_{\gamma}
is given by

(1.31)

\displaystyle\beta_{\gamma}=\left\{\begin{aligned} 0\,,\quad&\textrm{if }0\leq\gamma\leq 1\,,\\
-\tfrac{\gamma}{2}\,,\quad&\textrm{if }-3<\gamma<0\,,\end{aligned}\right.

which will be utilized later while designing the various weighted norms.

1.3.2.
Functions spaces

Based on the above weights, we introduce the spaces
X^{\infty}_{\beta,\vartheta}(A)
and
Y^{\infty}_{m,\beta,\vartheta}(A)
over
(x,v)\in{\Omega_{A}}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
for
m,\beta,\vartheta\in\mathbb{R}
and
0<A\leq\infty
with the weighted norms

(1.32)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{A;\beta,\vartheta}:=\|w_{\beta,\vartheta}f\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}=\sup_{(x,v)\in\Omega_{A}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}|w_{\beta,\vartheta}(v)f(x,v)|\,,\ [\![f]\!]_{A;m,\beta,\vartheta}:=\|\sigma_{x}^{\frac{m}{2}}w_{\beta,\vartheta}f\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}\,,

respectively. On the boundary
\Sigma:=\partial{\Omega_{A}}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we introduce the spaces
L^{\infty}_{\Sigma}
,
X^{\infty}_{\beta,\vartheta,\Sigma}
and
Y^{\infty}_{m,\beta,\vartheta,\Sigma}
endowed with the norms

(1.33)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{\Sigma}}:=\sup_{(x,v)\in\Sigma}|f(x,v)|\,,\ \|f\|_{\beta,\vartheta,\Sigma}:=\|w_{\beta,\vartheta}f\|_{L^{\infty}_{\Sigma}}\,,\ [\![f]\!]_{m,\beta,\vartheta,\Sigma}:=\|\sigma_{x}^{\frac{m}{2}}w_{\beta,\vartheta}f\|_{L^{\infty}_{\Sigma}}\,,

respectively. Let
L^{p}_{x}
and
L^{p}_{v}
with
1\leq p\leq\infty
be the standard
L^{p}
space over
x\in\Omega_{A}
and
v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, respectively. Moreover, the norm
\|\cdot\|_{L^{r}_{v}(L^{p}_{x})}
is defined by

\displaystyle\|\cdot\|_{L^{r}_{v}(L^{p}_{x})}=\|\big(\|f(\cdot,v)\|_{L^{x}_{p}}\big)\|_{L^{r}_{v}}\,.

We also introduce the space
L^{\infty}_{x}L^{2}_{v}
endowed with the norm

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}L^{2}_{v}}=\sup_{x\in\Omega_{A}}\|f(x,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}_{v}}\,.

Furthermore, the space
L^{2}_{x,v}
over
\Omega_{A}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
endows with the norm

(1.34)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{A}=\big(\iint_{\Omega_{A}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}|f(x,v)|^{2}\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,.

One introduces the out normal vector
n(0)=(0,0,-1)
and
n(A)=(0,0,1)
of the boundary
\partial\Omega_{A}
. Define

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\pm}=\{(x,v)\in\Sigma;\pm n(x)\cdot v>0\}\,.

We then define the space
{L^{2}_{\Sigma_{\pm}}}
over
(x,v)\in\Sigma_{\pm}
as follows:

(1.35)

\displaystyle\|f\|^{2}_{{L^{2}_{\Sigma_{\pm}}}}=\|f\|^{2}_{{L^{2}_{\Sigma_{\pm}^{0}}}}+\|f\|^{2}_{{L^{2}_{\Sigma_{\pm}^{A}}}}\,,\ \|f\|^{2}_{{L^{2}_{\Sigma_{\pm}^{0}}}}=\int_{\pm v_{3}<0}|f(0,v)|^{2}\mathrm{d}v\,,\ \|f\|^{2}_{{L^{2}_{\Sigma_{\pm}^{A}}}}=\int_{\pm v_{3}>0}|f(A,v)|^{2}\mathrm{d}v\,.

1.3.3.
Energy functionals

We now introduce total energy functional as

(1.36)

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}^{A}(g)=\mathscr{E}_{\infty}^{A}(g)+\mathscr{E}_{\mathtt{cro}}^{A}(g)+\big[\mathscr{E}_{\mathtt{NBE}}^{A}(g)\big]^{\frac{1}{2}}+\big[\mathscr{E}_{2}^{A}(g)\big]^{\frac{1}{2}}

for
0<A\leq\infty
. The weighted
L^{\infty}_{x,v}
energy functional
\mathscr{E}_{\infty}^{A}(g)
is defined as

(1.37)

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{\infty}^{A}(g)=[\![g]\!]_{A;m,\beta_{*},\vartheta}\,.

The cross energy functional
\mathscr{E}_{\mathtt{cro}}^{A}(g)
connecting the
L^{\infty}_{x,v}
and
L^{2}_{x,v}
estimates is defined as

(1.38)

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{\mathtt{cro}}^{A}(g)=\|\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}z_{-\alpha}\sigma_{x}^{\frac{m}{2}}w_{-\gamma+\beta_{\gamma},\vartheta}g\|_{A}+\|\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}}z_{-\alpha}\sigma_{x}^{\frac{m}{2}}z_{1}w_{-\gamma+\beta_{\gamma},\vartheta}\partial_{x}g\|_{A}\,.

The nondissipative boundary energy functional
\mathscr{E}_{\mathtt{NBE}}^{A}(g)
is expressed by

(1.39)

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{\mathtt{NBE}}^{A}(g)=

\displaystyle\Xi^{A}[\tfrac{59-24\gamma}{24(3-\gamma)};\tfrac{15}{8(3-\gamma)},0](g)+\Xi^{A}[\tfrac{9+\gamma}{3(3-\gamma)};\tfrac{7}{8(3-\gamma)},\beta_{\gamma}+\tfrac{1}{2}](g)+\Xi^{A}[\tfrac{107-24\gamma}{12(3-\gamma)};\tfrac{7}{4(3-\gamma)},0](g)

\displaystyle+\Xi^{A}[\tfrac{297-40\gamma}{36(3-\gamma)};\tfrac{3}{4(3-\gamma)},\beta_{\gamma}+\tfrac{1}{2}](g)+\Xi^{A}[\tfrac{189-52\gamma}{36(3-\gamma)};-\tfrac{1}{8(3-\gamma)},2\beta_{\gamma}+1](g)\,,

where the constant
\beta_{\gamma}
is given in (
1.31
), and

(1.40)

\displaystyle\Xi^{A}[\mathbf{a};\mathbf{b},\mathbf{c}](g):=

\displaystyle l^{\mathbf{a}}\Big(\delta\hbar\|(l^{-6}\delta x+l)^{\mathbf{b}}(\delta x+l)^{-\frac{1-\gamma}{2(3-\gamma)}}\mathcal{P}w_{\mathbf{c},0}g\|_{A}^{2}+\|(l^{-6}\delta x+l)^{\mathbf{b}}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{P}^{\perp}w_{\mathbf{c},0}g\|_{A}^{2}\Big)

with the weight
w_{\beta,\vartheta}
introduced in (
1.28
). The weighted
L^{2}_{x,v}
energy functional
\mathscr{E}_{2}^{A}(g)
is represented by

(1.41)

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{2}^{A}(g)=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\delta\hbar}\|(\delta x+l)^{-\frac{1-\gamma}{2(3-\gamma)}}\mathcal{P}w_{\beta,\vartheta}g\|_{A}^{2}+\|\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{P}^{\perp}w_{\beta,\vartheta}g\|_{A}^{2}\,.

We further introduce the source energy functional
\mathscr{A}^{A}(h)
and the boundary source energy functional
\mathscr{B}(\varphi)
as follows:

(1.42)

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}^{A}(h)=\mathscr{A}_{\infty}^{A}(h)+\mathscr{A}_{\mathtt{cro}}^{A}(h)+\big[\mathscr{A}_{\mathtt{NBE}}^{A}(h)\big]^{\frac{1}{2}}+\big[\mathscr{A}_{2}^{A}(h)\big]^{\frac{1}{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}(\varphi)=\mathscr{B}_{\infty}(\varphi)+\mathscr{B}_{\mathtt{cro}}(\varphi)+\big[\mathscr{B}_{\mathtt{NBE}}(\varphi)\big]^{\frac{1}{2}}+\big[\mathscr{B}_{2}(\varphi)\big]^{\frac{1}{2}}\,,

where

(1.43)

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{\infty}^{A}(h)=

\displaystyle[\![\nu^{-1}h]\!]_{A;m,\beta_{*},\vartheta}\,,\ \mathscr{A}_{\mathtt{cro}}^{A}(h)=\|\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}}z_{-\alpha}\sigma_{x}^{\frac{m}{2}}w_{-\gamma+\beta_{\gamma},\vartheta}h\|_{A}\,,

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{\mathtt{NBE}}^{A}(h)=

\displaystyle\Lambda^{A}[\tfrac{15}{8(3-\gamma)},0](h_{\sigma})+\Lambda^{A}[\tfrac{7}{4(3-\gamma)},0](h_{\sigma})+\Lambda^{A}[\tfrac{7}{8(3-\gamma)},\beta_{\gamma}+\tfrac{1}{2}](h_{\sigma})

\displaystyle+\Lambda^{A}[\tfrac{3}{4(3-\gamma)},\beta_{\gamma}+\tfrac{1}{2}](h_{\sigma})+\Lambda^{A}[-\tfrac{1}{8(3-\gamma)},2\beta_{\gamma}+1](h_{\sigma})\,,

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{2}^{A}(h)=

\displaystyle(\delta\hbar)^{-1}\|(\delta x+l)^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2(3-\gamma)}}\mathcal{P}w_{\beta,\vartheta}h\|_{A}^{2}+\|\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{P}^{\perp}w_{\beta,\vartheta}h\|_{A}^{2}\,,

and

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}_{\infty}(\varphi)=

\displaystyle\|\sigma_{x}^{\frac{m}{2}}(A,\cdot)w_{\beta,\vartheta}\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}}\,,\ \mathscr{B}_{2}(\varphi)=\||v_{3}|^{\frac{1}{2}}w_{\beta,\vartheta}\varphi\|_{L^{2}_{\Sigma_{-}^{A}}}^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}_{\mathtt{cro}}(\varphi)=

\displaystyle\||v_{3}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\sigma_{x}^{\frac{m}{2}}z_{-\alpha}w_{\beta+\beta_{\gamma},\vartheta}\varphi\|_{L^{2}_{\Sigma_{-}^{A}}}\,,

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}_{\mathtt{NBE}}(\varphi)=

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\star}[\tfrac{15}{8(3-\gamma)},0](\varphi_{A,\sigma})+\Lambda_{\star}[\tfrac{7}{4(3-\gamma)},0](\varphi_{A,\sigma})+\Lambda_{\star}[\tfrac{7}{8(3-\gamma)},\beta_{\gamma}+\tfrac{1}{2}](\varphi_{A,\sigma})

(1.44)

\displaystyle+\Lambda_{\star}[\tfrac{3}{4(3-\gamma)},\beta_{\gamma}+\tfrac{1}{2}](\varphi_{A,\sigma})+\Lambda_{\star}[-\tfrac{1}{8(3-\gamma)},2\beta_{\gamma}+1](\varphi_{A,\sigma})\,.

Here

\displaystyle\Lambda^{A}[\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}](h_{\sigma}):=(\delta\hbar)^{-1}\|(l^{-6}\delta x+l)^{\mathbf{a}}(\delta x+l)^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2(3-\gamma)}}\mathcal{P}w_{\mathbf{b},0}h_{\sigma}\|_{A}^{2}+\|(l^{-6}\delta x+l)^{\mathbf{a}}\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{P}^{\perp}w_{\mathbf{b},0}h_{\sigma}\|_{A}^{2}\,,

and

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\star}[\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}](\varphi_{A,\sigma}):=\||v_{3}|^{\frac{1}{2}}(l^{-6}\delta x+l)^{\mathbf{a}}w_{\mathbf{b},0}\varphi_{A,\sigma}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\Sigma_{-}^{A}}}\,.

At the end, we define the following norm associated with the boundary source term
\widetilde{f}_{b}(v)
as

(1.45)

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{f}_{b}\|_{\mathfrak{N}}:=

\displaystyle\|w_{1+\beta_{*},\vartheta+3\hbar}\widetilde{f}_{b}\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}}+\|\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}z_{-\alpha}w_{1-\gamma+\beta_{\gamma},\vartheta+3\hbar}\widetilde{f}_{b}\|_{L^{2}_{v}}+\|\mathcal{P}w_{1+\beta,\vartheta+3\hbar}\widetilde{f}_{b}\|_{L^{2}_{v}}

\displaystyle+\|\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}}z_{1-\alpha}w_{1-\gamma+\beta_{\gamma},\vartheta+3\hbar}\widetilde{f}_{b}\|_{L^{2}_{v}}+\|\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{P}^{\perp}w_{1+\beta,\vartheta+3\hbar}\widetilde{f}_{b}\|_{L^{2}_{v}}\,.

1.4.
Main results

1.4.1.
Linear problem (
KL
)

We now state the existence result on the Knudsen layer equation (
KL
). Before doing this, we first display the parameters assumptions occurred in
\mathscr{E}^{A}(g)
(see (
1.36
)) and
\mathscr{A}^{A}(h)
,
\mathscr{B}(\varphi)
(see (
1.42
)) above.

Parameters Hypotheses (PH):
For
\{\gamma,\alpha_{*},\beta,\beta_{*},\alpha,m,\delta,\hbar,\vartheta,l,\rho,T,T_{w},\mathfrak{u},u_{w}\}
,
we assume that

•

-3<\gamma\leq 1
,
0\leq\alpha_{*}\leq 1
,
m\geq 1
,
0<\delta<1
, integer
\beta_{*}\geq 0
;

•

\beta\geq 3(\beta_{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2})
, where
\beta_{\gamma}
is given in (
1.31
);

•

0<\alpha<\mu_{\gamma}
, where
\mu_{\gamma}>0
is given in Lemma
2.5
below;

•

0\leq\vartheta\leq\vartheta_{0}
, where small
\vartheta_{0}>0
is given in Lemma
4.1
below;

•

0<\hbar\leq o(1)\delta
and
l\geq O(1)(\delta\hbar)^{-\frac{512(3-\gamma)}{3(3+\gamma)}}
, where sufficiently small
0<o(1)\ll 1
and large enough
O(1)\gg 1
are both independent of
\delta,\hbar,l
;

•

\rho>0
,
\mathfrak{u},w_{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
\mathfrak{u}_{3}=u_{w3}=0
, and

(1.46)

\displaystyle 0<T_{w}<2T\,.

For the source term, we introduce a functional space
\mathfrak{X}_{\gamma}^{\infty}
by

(1.47)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}_{\gamma}^{\infty}=\big\{(S,f_{b});S\in\mathrm{Null}^{\perp}(\mathcal{L}),\mathscr{E}^{\infty}((\delta x+l)^{\frac{1-\gamma}{3-\gamma}}e^{\hbar\sigma}S)+\|{f}_{b}\|_{\mathfrak{N}}<\infty\big\}\,,

where the quantity
\mathscr{E}^{\infty}(\cdot)
is defined in (
1.36
) with
A=\infty
and the norm
\|{f}_{b}\|_{\mathfrak{N}}
is introduced in (
1.45
).

We now state the first result of this paper.

Theorem 1.1
.

Assume that

•

the mixed weight
\sigma(x,v)
be given in (
1.26
);

•

the parameters
\{\gamma,\alpha_{*},\beta,\beta_{*},\alpha,m,\delta,\hbar,\vartheta,l,\rho,T,T_{w},\mathfrak{u},u_{w}\}
satisfy the hypotheses
(PH)
;

•

the source terms
(S,f_{b})\in\mathbb{VSS}_{\alpha_{*}}\cap\mathfrak{X}_{\gamma}^{\infty}
, where
\mathbb{VSS}_{\alpha_{*}}
is defined in (
1.24
).

Then the linear Knudsen layer equation (
KL
) admits a unique mild solution
f(x,v)
satisfying

(1.48)

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}^{\infty}(e^{\hbar\sigma}f)\leq C(\mathscr{E}^{\infty}((\delta x+l)^{\frac{1-\gamma}{3-\gamma}}e^{\hbar\sigma}S)+\|{f}_{b}\|_{\mathfrak{N}})

for some constant
C>0
. Moreover, the set
\mathbb{VSS}_{\alpha_{*}}\cap\mathfrak{X}_{\gamma}^{\infty}
is the subspace of
\mathfrak{X}_{\gamma}^{\infty}
with codimension
4
.

Remark 1.1

(Exponential decay)
.

Since
\sigma(x,v)\geq c(\delta x+l)^{\frac{2}{3-\gamma}}
for
-3<\gamma\leq 1
, one has

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}^{\infty}(e^{\hbar\sigma}f)\geq\|\sigma_{x}^{\frac{m}{2}}w_{\beta,\vartheta}e^{\hbar\sigma}f\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}\geq C^{\prime}e^{\frac{1}{2}c(\delta x+l)^{\frac{2}{3-\gamma}}}e^{\frac{1}{2}\vartheta|v|^{2}}|f(x,v)|

uniformly in
(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Together with the bound (
1.48
) in Theorem
1.1
, the solution
f(x,v)
to the problem (
KL
) enjoys the pointwise decay behavior

(1.49)

\displaystyle|f(x,v)|\lesssim e^{-\frac{1}{2}c(\delta x+l)^{\frac{2}{3-\gamma}}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\vartheta|v|^{2}}\,.

Remark 1.2

(Background temperature
T
vs boundary temperature
T_{w}
)
.

The assumption (
1.46
), i.e.,
0<T_{w}<2T
, is such that the factor
\frac{M_{w}(v)}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}(v)}}
in the diffusive operator
\mathcal{D}_{w}
exponentially decays at
|v|\to+\infty
. Indeed,

\displaystyle\tfrac{M_{w}(v)}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}(v)}}=

\displaystyle\tfrac{\sqrt{2\pi}T^{\frac{3}{2}}}{\rho T_{w}^{2}}\mathrm{exp}\big\{-(\tfrac{1}{2T_{w}}-\tfrac{1}{4T})|v-u_{w}|^{2}-\tfrac{(u_{w}-\mathfrak{u})\cdot(v-u_{w})}{2T}-\tfrac{|u_{w}-\mathfrak{u}|^{2}}{4T}\big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\big(\mathrm{exp}\{-(\tfrac{1}{2T_{w}}-\tfrac{1}{4T})^{-}|v-u_{w}|^{2}\}\big)\to 0

as
|v|\to+\infty
under the assumption (
1.46
). This reasonable assumption is consistent with the desired exponential decay (
1.49
) about the Maxwell reflection boundary condition
f(0,v)|_{v_{3}>0}=(1-\alpha_{*})f(0,R_{0}v)+\alpha_{*}\tfrac{M_{w}(v)}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}(v)}}\int_{v_{3}^{\prime}<0}(-v_{3}^{\prime})f(0,v^{\prime})\sqrt{M}(v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}+f_{b}(v)
. In other words,
\tfrac{M_{w}(v)}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}(v)}}
should enjoy the same decay behavior of the quantity
f(0,v)|_{v_{3}>0}-(1-\alpha_{*})f(0,R_{0}v)-f_{b}(v)
.

Remark 1.3

(Re-characterization of the vanishing sources set
\mathbb{VSS}_{\alpha_{*}}
)
.

The structure of the vanishing sources set
\mathbb{VSS}_{\alpha_{*}}
given in (
1.24
) is unclear. Actually, it can be characterized by a clearer way. Let
\mathbb{I}_{\gamma}(S,f_{b})=f
be the solution operator of the following damped problem:

(1.50)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &v_{3}\partial_{x}f_{1}+\mathcal{L}f_{1}+\mathbf{D}f_{1}=(\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{P})S\,,\ v_{3}\partial_{x}f_{2}=\mathbb{P}S\,,\\
&f=f_{1}+f_{2}\,,f_{1}=(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})f\,,f_{2}=\mathcal{P}^{0}f\,,\\
&f(0,v)|_{v_{3}>0}=(1-\alpha_{*})f(0,R_{0}v)+\alpha_{*}\mathcal{D}_{w}f(0,v)+f_{b}(v)\,,\\
&\lim_{x\to+\infty}f_{1}(x,v)=\lim_{x\to+\infty}f_{2}(x,v)=0\,,\end{aligned}\right.

where the damping operator
\mathbf{D}
is given in (
1.63
) later. The solution to (
1.50
) is exactly that to (
KL
) if and only if
\mathbf{D}f_{1}(x,v)=0
for all
x\geq 0
and
v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, which is equivalent to (
5.46
) (replacing
f_{*}
by
f_{1}
) later. It is easy to see that the condition (
5.46
) can be equivalently expressed by

(1.51)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\psi_{3}^{*}\\
\widehat{\mathbb{B}}_{3}\\
\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{13}\\
\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{23}\end{array}\right)\Big(v_{3}f(0,v)+\int_{0}^{\infty}S(z,v)\mathrm{d}z\Big)\mathrm{d}v=0_{4}\,.

As a result, the set
\mathbb{VSS}_{\alpha_{*}}
can be re-characterized by

(1.52)

\displaystyle\mathbb{VSS}_{\alpha_{*}}=\big\{(S,f_{b});\mathbb{I}_{\gamma}(S,f_{b})=f\textrm{ such that \eqref{SBC} holds }\big\}\,.

1.4.2.
Application to nonlinear problem

In this part, we will employ the linear theory constructed in Theorem
1.1
to investigate the following nonlinear problem

(KL-NL)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &v_{3}\partial_{x}F=\mathcal{B}(F,F)+H\,,\ x>0,v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\,,\\
&F(0,v)|_{v_{3}>0}=(1-\alpha_{*})F(0,R_{0}v)+\alpha_{*}M_{w}(v)\int_{v_{3}^{\prime}<0}(-v_{3}^{\prime})F(0,v^{\prime})\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}+F_{b}(v)\,,\\
&\lim_{x\to+\infty}F(x,v)=\mathfrak{M}(v)\,.\end{aligned}\right.

Obviously,
T_{w}>0
and
u_{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
are the temperature and velocity of the boundary
\{x=0\}
. However,
T>0
and
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
are actually the temperature and velocity of the far-field boundary
\{x=+\infty\}
. Let
f=\tfrac{F-\mathfrak{M}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}}
,
\widehat{h}=\tfrac{H}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}}
and
\widehat{f}_{b}=\tfrac{F_{b}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}}
. Then the problem (
KL-NL
) can be equivalently rewritten as

(1.53)

{\small\left\{\begin{aligned} &v_{3}\partial_{x}f+\mathcal{L}f=\Gamma(f,f)+\widehat{h}\,,\ x>0\,,v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\,,\\
&f(0,v)|_{v_{3}>0}=(1-\alpha_{*})f(0,R_{0}v)+\alpha_{*}\mathcal{D}_{w}f(0,v)+\widehat{f}_{b}(v)\,,\ \lim_{x\to+\infty}f(x,v)=0\,,\end{aligned}\right.}

where the nonlinear operator
\Gamma(f,f)
is defined as

(1.54)

\displaystyle\Gamma(f,f)=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}}\mathcal{B}(f\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}},f\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}})\,.

As the same as the linear problem (
KL
), the far-field condition
\lim_{x\to+\infty}f(x,v)=0
in the nonlinear problem (
1.53
) is also overdetermined. We also introduce the vanishing sources set
\widetilde{\mathbb{VSS}}_{\alpha_{*}}
by

(1.55)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{VSS}}_{\alpha_{*}}=\big\{(\widehat{h},\widehat{f}_{b});\widehat{h}\in\mathrm{Null}^{\perp}(\mathcal{L})\textrm{ and }\widehat{f}_{b}\textrm{ in \eqref{KL-NL-f} such that }\lim_{x\to+\infty}f(x,v)=0\big\}\,.

For the source term, we introduce the functional space
\mathfrak{X}_{\gamma}^{\varsigma_{0}}
by

(1.56)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}_{\gamma}^{\varsigma_{0}}=\big\{(\widehat{h},\widehat{f}_{b});\widehat{h}\in\mathrm{Null}^{\perp}(\mathcal{L}),\varsigma:=\mathscr{E}^{\infty}((\delta x+l)^{\frac{1-\gamma}{3-\gamma}}e^{\hbar\sigma}\widehat{h})+\|\widehat{f}_{b}\|_{\mathfrak{N}}\leq\varsigma_{0}\big\}

for
\varsigma_{0}>0
, where the quantity
\mathscr{E}^{\infty}(\cdot)
is defined in (
1.36
) with
A=\infty
and the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathfrak{N}}
is introduced in (
1.45
).

More precisely, we can establish the following result.

Theorem 1.2
.

Assume that

•

the mixed weight
\sigma(x,v)
be given in (
1.26
);

•

the parameters
\{\gamma,\alpha_{*},\beta,\beta_{*},\alpha,m,\delta,\hbar,\vartheta,l,\rho,T,T_{w},\mathfrak{u},u_{w}\}
satisfy the hypotheses
(PH)
;

•

there is a small
\varsigma_{0}>0
such that
(\tfrac{H}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}},\tfrac{F_{b}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}})\in\widetilde{\mathbb{VSS}}_{\alpha_{*}}\cap\mathfrak{X}_{\gamma}^{\varsigma_{0}}
.

Then the nonlinear problem (
KL-NL
) admits a unique solution
F(x,v)
enjoying the bound

(1.57)

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}^{\infty}(e^{\hbar\sigma}\tfrac{F-\mathfrak{M}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}})\leq C\big[\mathscr{E}^{\infty}((\delta x+l)^{\frac{1-\gamma}{3-\gamma}}e^{\hbar\sigma}\tfrac{H}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}})+\|\tfrac{F_{b}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}}\|_{\mathfrak{N}}\big]

for some constant
C>0
, where the functionals
\mathscr{E}^{\infty}(\cdot)
is defined in (
1.36
) with
A=\infty
and the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathfrak{N}}
is given in (
1.45
). Moreover, the set
\widetilde{\mathbb{VSS}}_{\alpha_{*}}\cap\mathfrak{X}_{\gamma}^{\varsigma_{0}}
is the subspace of
\mathfrak{X}_{\gamma}^{\varsigma_{0}}
with codimension
4
.

Remark 1.4

(Re-characterization of the vanishing sources set
\widetilde{\mathbb{VSS}}_{\alpha_{*}}
)
.

As the similar as the linear problem, let
\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}(\widehat{h},\widehat{f}_{b})=f
be the solution operator of the following nonlinear damped problem:

(1.58)

{\small\left\{\begin{aligned} &v_{3}\partial_{x}f_{1}+\mathcal{L}f_{1}+\mathbf{D}f_{1}=(\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{P})\Gamma(f,f)+(\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{P})\widehat{h}\,,\ v_{3}\partial_{x}f_{2}=\mathbb{P}\Gamma(f,f)+\mathbb{P}\widehat{h}\,,f=f_{1}+f_{2}\,,\\
&f(0,v)|_{v_{3}>0}=(1-\alpha_{*})f(0,R_{0}v)+\alpha_{*}\mathcal{D}_{w}f(0,v)+\widehat{f}_{b}\,,\\
&\lim_{x\to+\infty}f_{1}(x,v)=\lim_{x\to+\infty}f_{2}(x,v)=0\,,\end{aligned}\right.}

where the artificial damping operator
\mathbf{D}
is defined in (
1.63
) later. The solution to (
1.58
) is exactly that to (
1.53
) (equivalently (
KL-NL
)) if and only if
\mathbf{D}f_{1}(x,v)=0
for all
x\geq 0
and
v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, which is further equivalent to the relations (
6.14
) later. As a result,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{VSS}}_{\alpha_{*}}=\Big\{

\displaystyle(\widehat{h},\widehat{f}_{b});\widehat{h}\in\mathrm{Null}^{\perp}(\mathcal{L}),\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}(\widehat{h},\widehat{f}_{b})=f\,,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\psi_{3}^{*}\\
\widehat{\mathbb{B}}_{3}\\
\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{13}\\
\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{23}\end{array}\right)\Big(v_{3}f(0,v)+\int_{0}^{\infty}\big[\Gamma(f,f)+\widehat{h}\big](z,v)\mathrm{d}z\Big)\mathrm{d}v=0_{4}\Big\}\,.

1.5.
Outline of existence of the solutions to the system
(
KL
)

We now sketch the rough process of solving the Knudsen layer equation (
KL
).

First, by the definitions of the operators
\mathcal{P}^{0}
in (
1.20
) and
\mathbb{P}
in (
1.21
), together with the relation
\mathbb{P}(v_{3}f)=v_{3}\mathcal{P}^{0}f
(see Lemma 3 of
[
7
]
),the equation (
KL
) can be decomposed as

(1.59)

\displaystyle v_{3}\partial_{x}\mathcal{P}^{0}f=\mathbb{P}S\,,\ \lim_{x\to+\infty}\mathcal{P}^{0}f=0\,,

and, by employing the notation
f_{*}=(\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{P}^{0})f
for simplicity,

(1.60)

{\small\left\{\begin{aligned} &v_{3}\partial_{x}f_{*}+\mathcal{L}f_{*}=(\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{P})S\,,\\
&f_{*}(0,v)|_{v_{3}>0}=(1-\alpha_{*})f_{*}(0,R_{0}v)+\alpha_{*}\mathcal{D}_{w}f_{*}(0,v)+\widetilde{f}_{b}(v)\,,\ \lim_{x\to+\infty}f_{*}(x,v)=0\,,\end{aligned}\right.}

where

(1.61)

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{b}(v)=f_{b}(v)-\mathcal{P}^{0}f(0,v)\mathbf{1}_{v_{3}>0}+(1-\alpha_{*})\mathcal{P}^{0}f(0,R_{0}v)\mathbf{1}_{v_{3}>0}+\alpha_{*}\mathcal{D}_{w}\mathcal{P}^{0}f(0,v)\mathbf{1}_{v_{3}>0}\,.

The equation (
1.59
) is actually an ordinary differential equation, which can be explicitly solved by

(1.62)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{0}f(x,v)=-\int_{x}^{+\infty}\tfrac{1}{v_{3}}\mathbb{P}S(x^{\prime},v)\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\,.

Then the function
\widetilde{f}_{b}(v)
is determined by the boundary source term
f_{b}(v)
and part of source term
\mathbb{P}S
. Then we will focus on the problem (
1.60
) later.

Second, because the operator
\mathcal{L}
is coercive merely on the orthogonal space
\mathrm{Null}^{\perp}(\mathcal{L})
, we should add an artificial damping on the fluid part
\mathrm{Null}(\mathcal{L})
to the problem (
1.60
). Namely, we consider the following damped problem

(KLd)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &v_{3}\partial_{x}f_{*}+\mathcal{L}f_{*}+\mathbf{D}f_{*}=(\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{P})S\,,\\
&f_{*}(0,v)|_{v_{3}>0}=(1-\alpha_{*})f_{*}(0,R_{0}v)+\alpha_{*}\mathcal{D}_{w}f_{*}(0,v)+\widetilde{f}_{b}(v)\,,\ \lim_{x\to+\infty}f_{*}(x,v)=0\,,\end{aligned}\right.

where the artificial damping operator
\mathbf{D}f_{*}
is defined as

(1.63)

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}f_{*}=\ss_{+}(\delta x+l)^{-\frac{1-\gamma}{3-\gamma}}\mathcal{P}^{+}(v_{3}f_{*})+\ss_{0}(\delta x+l)^{-\frac{1-\gamma}{3-\gamma}}\mathcal{P}^{0}f_{*}

for all
f_{*}\in L^{2}_{x,v}
. Here the constants
\ss_{+},\ss_{0}>0
will be determined later. We remark that the artificial damping penalizes the decay of subspace of
\mathrm{Null}(\mathcal{L})
associated with the nonnegative eigenvalues of the operator
\mathcal{A}
given in (
1.14
). Once one justifies the existence of the equation (
KLd
), the artificial damping will be removed by employing the structure of the vanishing sources set
\mathbb{VSS}_{\alpha_{*}}
so that the existence of the problem (
1.60
) is established.

Third, we homogenize the Maxwell reflection boundary condition in (
KLd
). Let
\Upsilon:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to[0,1]
be a smooth monotone function satisfying

(1.64)

\displaystyle\Upsilon(x)=\left\{\begin{aligned} 1\quad&\textrm{for }\ 0\leq x\leq 1\\
0\quad&\textrm{for }\ x\geq 2\,.\end{aligned}\right.

Denote by

(1.65)

\displaystyle g(x,v)=

\displaystyle f_{*}(x,v)-\Upsilon(x)\widetilde{f}_{b}(v)\,,

\displaystyle h(x,v)=

\displaystyle(\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{P})S(x,v)-v_{3}\partial_{x}\Upsilon(x)\widetilde{f}_{b}(v)-\Upsilon(x)(\mathcal{L}+\mathbf{D})\widetilde{f}_{b}(v)\,.

It is easy to see that

\displaystyle v_{3}\partial_{x}g+\mathcal{L}g+\mathbf{D}g=h\,.

Recall that
f_{b}(v)=0
for
v_{3}<0
, which means that
\widetilde{f}_{b}(v)|_{v_{3}<0}=0
and
\widetilde{f}_{b}(R_{0}v)|_{v_{3}>0}=0
. Then

\displaystyle g(0,R_{0}v)|_{v_{3}>0}=f_{*}(0,R_{0}v)|_{v_{3}>0}-\Upsilon(0)\widetilde{f}_{b}(R_{0}v)|_{v_{3}>0}=f_{*}(0,R_{0}v)|_{v_{3}>0}\,.

Moreover,
g(0,v)|_{v_{3}>0}=f_{*}(0,v)|_{v_{3}>0}-\Upsilon(0)\widetilde{f}_{b}(v)|_{v_{3}>0}=f_{*}(0,v)-\widetilde{f}_{b}(v))|_{v_{3}>0}
, where the fact
\Upsilon(0)=1
has been used. It is further derived that

\displaystyle g(0,v^{\prime})|_{v_{3}^{\prime}<0}=f_{*}(0,v^{\prime})|_{v_{3}^{\prime}<0}-\Upsilon(0)\widetilde{f}_{b}(v^{\prime})|_{v_{3}^{\prime}<0}=f_{*}(0,v^{\prime})|_{v_{3}^{\prime}<0}\,,

which infers that
\mathcal{D}_{w}g(0,v)=\mathcal{D}_{w}f_{*}(0,v)
. As a result,
g(x,v)
satisfies the boundary condition

\displaystyle g(0,v)|_{v_{3}>0}=(1-\alpha_{*})g(0,R_{0}v)+\alpha_{*}\mathcal{D}_{w}g(0,v)\,.

Observing that
\Upsilon(x)=0
for
x\geq 2
, one has
\lim_{x\to+\infty}g(x,v)=\lim_{x\to+\infty}f_{*}(x,v)=0
. Therefore,
g(x,v)
satisfies

(1.66)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &v_{3}\partial_{x}g+\mathcal{L}g+\mathbf{D}g=h\,,\\
&g(0,v)|_{v_{3}>0}=(1-\alpha_{*})g(0,R_{0}v)+\alpha_{*}\mathcal{D}_{w}g(0,v)\,,\ \lim_{x\to+\infty}g(x,v)=0\,,\end{aligned}\right.

where
h(x,v)
is defined in (
1.65
). So, the problem (
1.66
) is the equivalent form of the damped problem (
KLd
).

Forth, in order to prove the existence of (
1.66
), we consider the problem in a finite slab with Maxwell reflection condition at
x=0
and incoming boundary condition at
x=A
. We call it the so-called connection auxiliary equation. Namely, for
(x,v)\in\Omega_{A}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
\Omega_{A}=\{x;0<x<A\}
,

(CA-eq)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &v_{3}\partial_{x}g+\mathcal{L}g+\mathbf{D}g=h\,,\\
&g(0,v)|_{v_{3}>0}=(1-\alpha_{*})g(0,R_{0}v)+\alpha_{*}\mathcal{D}_{w}g(0,v)\,,\ g(A,v)|_{v_{3}<0}=\varphi_{A}(v)\,.\end{aligned}\right.

The incoming data
g(A,v)|_{v_{3}<0}=0
is the most closed approximation of the far-field condition
\lim_{x\to+\infty}g(x,v)=0
in (
1.66
). Here we write down the general
\varphi_{A}(v)
will be used to prove the corresponding uniqueness results. We will prove the solution of the approximate problem (
CA-eq
) converges to that of (
1.66
) with
\varphi_{A}=0
as
A\to+\infty
. The existence of the problem (
CA-eq
) will be shown by employing the Hanh-Banach Theorem and the Lax-Milgram Theorem.

1.6.
Methodology and novelties

In this subsection, we methodologically sketch the proof of the main results and illustrate the novelties of this paper. As shown in the previous subsection, the core of current work is to derive the uniform estimates of the connection auxiliary equation (
CA-eq
) associated with the parameter
A>1
. The main ideas are displayed as follows.

(I) Choice of the mixed weight
\sigma(x,v)
.
The major part of the equation (
KL
) reads

(1.67)

\displaystyle v_{3}\partial_{x}f+\nu(v)f=s.o.t.\textrm{ (some other terms)}\,.

For the hard sphere model
\gamma=1
,
v_{3}
and
\nu(v)
have the same order as
|v|\gg 1
, i.e.
|v_{3}|\lesssim\nu(v)
. Roughly speaking, the
x
-decay can be expected from

\displaystyle\partial_{x}f+cf=s.o.t.\,,

which means that the expected
x
-decay is
e^{-cx}
. For example, for the hard sphere model
\gamma=1
, there have been some related works as follows: Golse-Perthame-Sulem
[
8
]
proved the exponential decay
e^{-cx}
in the space
L^{2}(e^{cx}\mathrm{d}x;L^{2}((1+|v|)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{d}v))\cap L^{\infty}(e^{cx}\mathrm{d}x;L^{2}(\mathrm{d}v))
for specular reflection condition (
\alpha_{*}=0
); Coron-Golse-Sulem
[
4
]
verified the exponential decay
e^{-cx}
in
L^{\infty}(e^{cx}\mathrm{d}x;L^{2}(|v_{3}|\mathrm{d}v))
for general Maxwell reflection boundary condition (
0\leq\alpha_{*}\leq 1
); Huang-Wang
[
15
]
proved the exponential decay
e^{-cx}e^{-a|v|^{2}}
in
L^{\infty}_{x,v}
space for diffusive reflection condition (
\alpha_{*}=1
); Huang-Jiang-Wang
[
14
]
also shown the exponential decay
e^{-cx}e^{-a|v|^{2}}
in
L^{\infty}_{x,v}
space for specular reflection condition (
\alpha_{*}=0
); He-Jiang-Wu
[
13
]
recently proved the exponential decay
e^{-cx}e^{-a|v|^{2}}
in
L^{\infty}_{x,v}
space for Maxwell reflection boundary condition (
0<\alpha_{*}\leq 1
). All of previous works have essentially used the fact
|v_{3}|\lesssim\nu(v)
which only holds for
\gamma=1
.

For the cases
-3<\gamma<1
,
|v_{3}|\lesssim\nu(v)|v_{3}|^{1-\gamma}
. Note that (
1.67
) implies

\displaystyle\partial_{x}(e^{\frac{\nu(v)}{v_{3}}x}f)=s.o.t.\,.

This inspires us to introduce an
(x,v)
-mixed weight
\sigma(x,v)
to deal with the power
\frac{\nu(v)}{v_{3}}x
. More precisely, (
1.67
) reduces to

\displaystyle v_{3}\partial_{x}(e^{\hbar\sigma}f)+(\nu(v)-\hbar v_{3}\sigma_{x})e^{\hbar\sigma}f=s.o.t.\,,

which inspires us to find a weight
\sigma(x,v)
such that
v_{3}\sigma_{x}(x,v)\thicksim\nu(v)
. As in Chen-Liu-Yang’s work
[
3
]
, the weight
\sigma(x,v)
in (
1.26
) is introduced, which satisfies

|v_{3}|\sigma_{x}(x,v)\lesssim\nu(v)\,,\sigma(x,v)\geq c(\delta x+l)^{\frac{2}{3-\gamma}}\,,\sigma_{x}(x,v)\lesssim(\delta x+l)^{-\frac{1-\gamma}{3-\gamma}}\,.

The derivative
\sigma_{x}
of
\sigma
actually balances the disparity of
|v_{3}|
and
\nu(v)
. We remark that the work
[
3
]
investigated the Knudsen layer equation with the nondegenerate moving boundary condition and hard potential collision kernel
0<\gamma\leq 1
. Here we, similarly as in
[
3
]
, design the mixed weight
\sigma(x,v)
in (
1.26
) to verify the exponential decays both
x
and
v
variables for all cases
-3<\gamma\leq 1
and
0\leq\alpha_{*}\leq 1
.

The intuition idea of choosing the mixed weight
\sigma(x,v)
can be illustrated as follows:
1
1

1

This intuition idea comes from the valuable discussion with Prof. Tong Yang.
We set

\displaystyle\sigma(x,v)=\underbrace{5(\delta x+l)^{\gamma_{1}}\big(1-\Upsilon(\tfrac{\delta x+l}{(1+|v-\mathfrak{u}|)^{\gamma_{2}}})\big)}_{\sigma_{I}}+\underbrace{\big(\tfrac{\delta x+l}{(1+|v-\mathfrak{u}|)^{\gamma_{3}}}+3|v-\mathfrak{u}|^{2}\big)\Upsilon(\tfrac{\delta x+l}{(1+|v-\mathfrak{u}|)^{\gamma_{2}}})}_{\sigma_{I\!I}}\,,

where the indices
\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{2}
and
\gamma_{3}
will be determined later. The first part
\sigma_{I}
depends only
x
when
x\gg 1
because we want to show decay like
e^{-\sigma}=e^{-c(\delta x+l)^{\gamma_{1}}}
. The factor
3|v-\mathfrak{u}|^{2}
in the second part
\sigma_{I\!I}
bears the best (Maxwellian type) decay in
v
as
|v|\gg 1
. The factor
\tfrac{\delta x+l}{(1+|v-\mathfrak{u}|)^{\gamma_{3}}}
in the second part
\sigma_{I\!I}
recovers the relation between
\sigma_{x}
and
v
-polynomials, i.e.,
\sigma_{x}\thicksim(1+|v-\mathfrak{u}|)^{-\gamma_{3}}
, which will be applied to balance the disparity of
|v_{3}|
and
\nu(v)
. Let
g=e^{\sigma}f
. The equation (
1.67
) can be rewritten as

(1.68)

\displaystyle v_{3}\partial_{x}g+(-v_{3}\sigma_{x}+\nu(v))g=s.o.t.\,.

Because higher order dissipation in
v
than
\nu(v)\thicksim(1+|v|)^{\gamma}
can not be expected, we set
v_{3}\sigma_{x}\thicksim\nu(v)
, which means that
\sigma_{x}\thicksim(1+|v|)^{\gamma-1}
. Together with
\sigma_{x}\thicksim(1+|v-\mathfrak{u}|)^{-\gamma_{3}}
, one has

(1.69)

\displaystyle-\gamma_{3}=\gamma-1\,.

In the transition regime
\delta x+l\thicksim(1+|v-\mathfrak{u}|)^{\gamma_{2}}
, one should have
\tfrac{\delta x+l}{(1+|v-\mathfrak{u}|)^{\gamma_{3}}}\thicksim|v-\mathfrak{u}|^{2}
. This indicates that

(1.70)

\displaystyle\gamma_{2}-\gamma_{3}=2\,.

Moreover, the ODE (
1.68
) in the transition regime is like

\displaystyle\partial_{x}g+(1+|v-\mathfrak{u}|)^{\gamma-1}g\thicksim\partial_{x}g+(\delta x+l)^{\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma_{2}}}g=s.o.t.\,,

which reveals that the decay rate in
x
should be
g\thicksim e^{-(\delta x+l)^{\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma_{2}}+1}}
. Consequently, one obtains

(1.71)

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}=\tfrac{\gamma-1}{\gamma_{2}}+1\,.

Then the relations (
1.69
)-(
1.70
)-(
1.71
) imply that
\gamma_{1}=\frac{2}{3-\gamma}
,
\gamma_{2}=3-\gamma
and
\gamma_{3}=1-\gamma
. The mixed weight
\sigma(x,v)
in (
1.26
) is therefore constructed.

(II) Artificial damping.
It is well-known that the linearized Boltzmann operator
\mathcal{L}
does not supply the coercivity structure in the null space
\mathrm{Null}(\mathcal{L})
, i.e., macroscopic damping effect. In the previous literature, various artificial damping quantities are introduced to obtain the macroscopic coercivity, and then remove additional damping quantities by proper ways. For example, in the works
[
4
,
8
,
13
,
14
,
15
]
, the damping
\varepsilon f
was introduced to deal with the linear Knudsen layer equation for hard sphere collision case with various boundary conditions. Then it is removed by taking
\varepsilon\to 0
via compact arguments. Moreover, in the works
[
3
,
26
,
27
,
28
]
associated with the incoming data boundary condition, the artificial damping
-\gamma P_{0}^{+}\xi_{1}f
(
\gamma>0
) coming from the eigenspace corresponding to positive eigenvalues of the linear operator
P_{0}\xi_{1}P_{0}
are applied to deal with the incoming boundary conditions. In order to return the original equation, a further assumption on the incoming data
a(\xi)
(the data
a(\xi)
vanishes in the eigenspace corresponding to positive eigenvalues of the linear operator
P_{0}\xi_{1}P_{0}
, i.e.,
P_{0}^{+}\xi_{1}P_{0}a=0
) will be imposed such that the artificial damping
-\gamma P_{0}^{+}\xi_{1}f
vanishes by the Grönwall inequality argument. Remark that the series of works
[
3
,
26
,
27
,
28
]
considered the nondegenerate case, i.e., the far-field velocity such that the operator
P_{0}\xi_{1}P_{0}
admits no zero eigenvalue. For the corresponding degenerate case in the hard sphere model (
\gamma=1
), Golse
[
7
]
studied the
L^{\infty}
decay theory of the Knudsen layer problem by adding a damping
\alpha\Pi_{+}(v_{1}\cdot)+\beta\mathbf{p}-\gamma v_{1}I
for some constants
\alpha,\beta,\gamma>0
, where
\Pi_{+}
is the orthogonal projection on the positive eigensubspace,
\mathbf{p}
is that on the zero eigensubspace, and
-v_{1}I
supplies the damping effect on the negative eigensubspace.

Inspired by Golse’s work, we add the artificial damping
\mathbf{D}f_{*}
given in (
1.63
) in the problem (
KLd
), i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}f_{*}=\ss_{+}(\delta x+l)^{-\frac{1-\gamma}{3-\gamma}}\mathcal{P}^{+}(v_{3}f_{*})+\ss_{0}(\delta x+l)^{-\frac{1-\gamma}{3-\gamma}}\mathcal{P}^{0}f_{*}\,.

Here
\mathcal{P}+
and
\mathcal{P}^{0}
supply the damping effect in the positive and zero eigensubspaces, respectively. What different with Golse’s damping is that we do not add a damping effect on the negative eigensubspace. The reason is that there is an intrinsic damping mechanism in the negative eigensubspace, see (
9.18
) later, hence,

\displaystyle-\tfrac{10\delta\hbar}{3-\gamma}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}v_{3}(a_{4}^{*}\psi_{4}^{*})^{2}\mathrm{d}v=\sqrt{\tfrac{3}{5T}}\tfrac{10\delta\hbar}{3-\gamma}(a_{4}^{*})^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}v_{3}\psi_{4}^{*}\mathcal{P}v_{3}\mathcal{P}\psi_{4}^{*}\mathrm{d}v\geq\mu_{2}\delta\hbar(a_{4}^{*})^{2}\,,

where
\mathcal{P}v_{3}\mathcal{P}\psi_{4}^{*}=-\sqrt{\frac{5}{3}T}\psi_{4}^{*}
, and
\psi_{4}^{*}
is the negative unit eigenvector of the eigenvalue
\lambda_{4}=-\sqrt{\frac{5}{3}T}<0
of the operator
\mathcal{P}v_{3}\mathcal{P}
(see (
1.16
)). For the precise application, we first rescale the equation (
CA-eq
) by using the factor
e^{\hbar\sigma}
, (
CA-eq
) can be equivalently expressed by (
3.1
) below, i.e.,

\displaystyle v_{3}\partial_{x}g+\mathbf{D}_{\hbar}g-\hbar\sigma_{x}v_{3}g+\nu(v)g-\lambda K_{\hbar}g=h\,.

The term
\nu(v)g-\lambda K_{\hbar}g
supplies the coercivity structure
\|\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{P}^{\perp}w_{\beta,\vartheta}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{v}}
in
\mathrm{Null}^{\perp}(\mathcal{L})
. By using the property of
\sigma(x,v)
and the damping mechanism of
\mathbf{D}
, the term
\mathbf{D}_{\hbar}g-\hbar\sigma_{x}v_{3}g
implies the macroscopic damping
\hbar(\delta x+l)^{-\frac{1-\gamma}{3-\gamma}}\|\mathcal{P}w_{\beta,\vartheta}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{v}}
by losing a small microscopic quantity
\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{P}^{\perp}w_{\beta,\vartheta}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{v}}
(the smallness comes from the small parameter
\hbar>0
). To be more precise, the macroscopic damping is constructed in Lemma
2.6
below, i.e.,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}w^{2}_{\beta,\vartheta}(\mathbf{D}_{\hbar}g-\hbar\sigma_{x}v_{3}g)\mathrm{d}v\geq\mu_{0}\delta\hbar(\delta x+l)^{-\frac{1-\gamma}{3-\gamma}}\|\mathcal{P}w_{\beta,\vartheta}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{v}}-C\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{P}^{\perp}w_{\beta,\vartheta}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{v}}\,.

(III) Nondissipative boundary condition.
While multiplying the equation (
1.66
) by
g(x,v)
and integrating the resultant equation over
(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, one can obtain the boundary
L^{2}
energy

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\!B\!C}:=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}v_{3}|g(0,v)|^{2}\mathrm{d}v\,.

If the boundary condition
g(0,v)|_{v_{3}>0}=\mathcal{K}g(0,v)
is such that
\mathcal{E}_{\!B\!C}\geq 0
, we call that the boundary condition
g(0,v)|_{v_{3}>0}=\mathcal{K}g(0,v)
is
dissipative
. Otherwise, we call that the corresponding boundary is
nondissipative
. In this paper, we consider

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}g(0,v)=(1-\alpha_{*})g(0,R_{0}v)+\alpha_{*}\tfrac{M_{w}(v)}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}(v)}}\int_{v_{3}^{\prime}<0}(-v_{3}^{\prime})g(0,v^{\prime})\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}(v^{\prime})}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\,.

To the best knowledge of the authors, all the known results of the boundary value problem of Boltzmann equation studied the dissipative boundary or nearly dissipative boundary. For instance, Guo
[
10
]
studied the Boltzmann equation in three dimensional bounded domain with in-flow, bounce-back, specular reflection (
\alpha_{*}=0
) and diffusive reflection (
\alpha_{*}=1
) boundary conditions associated with the global Maxwellian
M_{w}(v)=\mathfrak{M}(v)=e^{-\frac{|v|^{2}}{2}}
. We remark that the in-flow boundary condition responds to the case
\mathcal{K}=0
by homogenizing the boundary condition as the similar operations as in Subsection
1.5
above. Li-Lu-Sun
[
20
]
investigated one dimensional half-space linear steady Boltzmann equation with general dissipative boundary condition. Moreover, the all hard sphere model
\gamma=1
mentioned in Part (I) and (II) of this subsection studied the dissipative Maxwell type boundary conditions. Moreover, Esposito-Guo-Kim-Marra
[
6
]
studied the steady Boltzmann equation of hard potential model (
0\leq\gamma\leq 1
) with non-isothermal diffusive boundary condition (
\alpha_{*}=1
) in the bounded spatial domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
, in which the boundary temperature
\theta=\theta(x)
for
x\in\partial\Omega
. For general
\theta=\theta(x)
, the diffusive boundary condition is of course nondissipative. However, for a fixed constant
\theta_{0}>0
, there was an assumption
\sup_{x\in\partial\Omega}|\theta(x)-\theta_{0}|<\delta_{0}
in
[
6
]
for a sufficiently small
\delta_{0}>0
. It is actually a nearly dissipative boundary. The ideas in
[
6
]
were to avoid the boundary
L^{2}
energy
\mathcal{E}_{\!B\!C}
by employing the trajectory approach. They first controlled the weighted
L^{\infty}
norm. Then the required
L^{2}
norm can be obtained by the
L^{\infty}
estimates, see Lemma 3.1 of
[
6
]
. However, this method essentially relied on the boundedness of the domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
. In our work, the spatial domain
\mathbb{R}_{+}
is unbounded, so that the approach of
[
6
]
do not work for our issue.

We now focus on the Maxwell reflection boundary condition of (
1.66
) considered in our paper. Actually, under the boundary condition in (
1.66
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\!B\!C}=

\displaystyle 2\alpha_{*}(1-\alpha_{*})\Big[\int_{v_{3}<0}|v_{3}|g^{2}(0,v)\mathrm{d}v-\int_{v_{3}<0}|v_{3}|\tfrac{M_{w}}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}}}g(0,v)\mathrm{d}v\int_{v_{3}<0}|v_{3}|g(0,v)\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}(v)}\mathrm{d}v\Big]

\displaystyle+\alpha_{*}^{2}\Big[\int_{v_{3}<0}|v_{3}|g^{2}(0,v)\mathrm{d}v-\int_{v_{3}<0}|v_{3}|\tfrac{M_{w}^{2}}{\mathfrak{M}}\mathrm{d}v\big(\int_{v_{3}<0}|v_{3}|g(0,v)\sqrt{\mathfrak{M}(v)}\mathrm{d}v\big)^{2}\Big]\,.

If
\alpha_{*}=0
,
\mathcal{E}_{\!B\!C}=0
, which means that the specular reflection boundary condition is dissipative. If
0<\alpha_{*}\leq 1
, the fact
\mathcal{E}_{\!B\!C}\geq 0
for any
g(0,v)
is equivalent to dissipative condition

(1.72)

\displaystyle\int_{v_{3}<0}|v_{3}|\tfrac{M_{w}^{2}}{\mathfrak{M}}\mathrm{d}v\int_{v_{3}<0}|v_{3}|\mathfrak{M}\mathrm{d}v\leq 1\,.

However, the general parameters
(T,T_{w},\mathfrak{u},u_{w})
given in the hypotheses
(PH)
above do not meet the dissipative condition (
1.72
). It is easy to check that (
1.72
) will hold by setting
T=T_{w}
and
\mathfrak{u}=u_{w}
. In the works
[
4
,
8
,
10
,
13
,
15
]
, the special case
(\mathfrak{u},T)=(u_{w},T_{w})=(\vec{0},1)
, which subjected to the dissipative condition (
1.72
), was considered.

In our work, we focus on the general case WITHOUT the dissipative condition (
1.72
). We only assume that
\{T,\mathfrak{u},T_{w},u_{w}\}
satisfies the hypotheses given in
(PH)
, hence,

\displaystyle 0<T_{w}<2T\,,u_{w},\mathfrak{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\,,u_{w3}=\mathfrak{u}_{3}=0\,.

So we do not require that the temperature and velocity of the boundaries
\{x=0\}
and
\{x=+\infty\}
are closed each other. Our way will be divided into the following three steps.

(III.1) Nondissipative boundary lemma.
The nondissipation of
\mathcal{E}_{\!B\!C}
comes from the integral form
\int_{v_{3}<0}|v_{3}|g(0,v)\mathfrak{w}(v)\mathrm{d}v
for some exponential decay function
\mathfrak{w}(v)
. In order to dominate the previous integral form, in the so-called nondissipative boundary lemma (see Lemma
3.1
below), we subtly construct a useful nondissipative boundary inequality

\displaystyle|\int_{v_{3}<0}|v_{3}|\mathfrak{w}(v)g_{\sigma}(0,v)\mathrm{d}v|\lesssim

\displaystyle\delta^{-\frac{1}{2}}l^{-(a-\frac{1-\gamma}{2(3-\gamma)})}\|(l^{-\mathbf{Z}}\delta x+l)^{a}(\delta x+l)^{-\frac{1-\gamma}{2(3-\gamma)}}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}g_{\sigma}\|_{A}

\displaystyle+\delta^{-\frac{1}{2}}l^{-50}\|(\delta x+l)^{-\frac{1-\gamma}{2(3-\gamma)}}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}g_{\sigma}\|_{A}+\cdots

for any
a>0
and
\mathbf{Z}\geq 0
. Once
l\geq 1
is large enough such that
\delta^{-\frac{1}{2}}l^{-50}
is sufficiently small, the quantity
\delta^{-\frac{1}{2}}l^{-50}\|(\delta x+l)^{-\frac{1-\gamma}{2(3-\gamma)}}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}g_{\sigma}\|_{A}
can be easily dominated by the coercivity of
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathbf{D}
. The degree of freedom for the constants
a>0
and
\mathbf{Z}\geq 0
will play an essential role in controlling the quantity
\delta^{-\frac{1}{2}}l^{-(a-\frac{1-\gamma}{2(3-\gamma)})}\|(l^{-\mathbf{Z}}\delta x+l)^{a}(\delta x+l)^{-\frac{1-\gamma}{2(3-\gamma)}}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}g_{\sigma}\|_{A}
. The idea is to pull the boundary integral
\int_{v_{3}<0}|v_{3}|\mathfrak{w}(v)g_{\sigma}(0,v)\mathrm{d}v
to the interior of the equation by the Euler factor
e^{-(\delta x+l)}
. When
|v-\mathfrak{u}|\geq 2
, the integral can be bounded by the second quantity with small factor
\delta^{-\frac{1}{2}}l^{-50}
, where the smallness comes from the interaction of the
x
- and
v
-variables, see the estimate of
U_{11}
in (
3.6
) later. If
|v-\mathfrak{u}|\leq 2
, the integral can be bounded by the first quantity with small factor
\delta^{-\frac{1}{2}}l^{-(a-\frac{1-\gamma}{2(3-\gamma)})}
. The key is to use the relation
\mathbf{1}_{|v-\mathfrak{u}|\leq 2}|v-\mathfrak{u}|\leq 2(l^{-\mathbf{Z}-1}\delta x+1)^{a}
, which also guarantees the degree of freedom of the parameters
\mathbf{Z}\geq 0
and
a>0
.

By utilizing the above inequality, we can establish the Boundary energy lemma (see Lemma
3.2
below). Namely, the boundary energy
\mathcal{E}_{\!B\!C}
with a certain weight
w_{*}(v)
has a lower bound

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\!B\!C}\geq

\displaystyle[1-(1-\alpha_{*})^{2}]\int_{v_{3}<0}|v_{3}|w_{*}(v)g^{2}_{\sigma}(0,v)\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle-C_{0}\alpha_{*}\delta^{-1}l^{-2(a-\frac{1-\gamma}{2(3-\gamma)})}\|(l^{-\mathbf{Z}}\delta x+l)^{a}(\delta x+l)^{-\frac{1-\gamma}{2(3-\gamma)}}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}g_{\sigma}\|_{A}^{2}-\alpha_{*}\times(\textrm{some controllable quantities})

for any
a>0
and
\mathbf{Z}\geq 0
. Then the weighted
L^{2}
estimates implies

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{2}^{A}(g_{\sigma})\lesssim\delta^{-1}l^{-2(a-\frac{1-\gamma}{2(3-\gamma)})}\|(l^{-\mathbf{Z}}\delta x+l)^{a}(\delta x+l)^{-\frac{1-\gamma}{2(3-\gamma)}}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}g_{\sigma}\|_{A}^{2}+\cdots\,.

(III.2) Spatial-velocity indices iteration approach.
In the quantity

(1.73)

\displaystyle\delta^{-1}l^{-2(a-\frac{1-\gamma}{2(3-\gamma)})}\|(l^{-\mathbf{Z}}\delta x+l)^{a}(\delta x+l)^{-\frac{1-\gamma}{2(3-\gamma)}}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}g_{\sigma}\|_{A}^{2}\,,

there is an additional spatial polynomial weight
(l^{-\mathbf{Z}}\delta x+l)^{a}
with positive power
a>0
. In order to overcome this difficulty, we develop a so-called spatial-velocity indices iteration approach. The ideas is to shift the spatial polynomial weight to the velocity one in the
L^{2}_{x,v}
framework. As in Lemma
3.4
below, together with the Nondissipative boundary lemma, the following spatial-velocity indices iteration form is found:

(1.74)

\displaystyle\psi_{\mathbf{Z};\mathtt{q},\beta_{\sharp}}\lesssim

\displaystyle l^{-\frac{2}{3-\gamma}\mathbf{Z}}\psi_{\mathbf{Z};\mathtt{q}-\frac{1}{3-\gamma},\beta_{\sharp}+\beta_{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2}}+\alpha_{*}l^{2\mathtt{q}-(2a-\frac{1-\gamma}{3-\gamma})}\psi_{\mathbf{Z};a,0}+\textrm{ some controllable terms}\,,

for any
a>0
and
\mathbf{Z}\geq 0
, where
\psi_{\mathbf{Z};\mathtt{q},\beta_{\sharp}}
is defined in (
3.30
), i.e.,

\psi_{\mathbf{Z};\mathtt{q},\beta_{\sharp}}:=\delta\hbar\|(l^{-\mathbf{Z}}\delta x+l)^{\mathtt{q}}(\delta x+l)^{-\frac{1-\gamma}{2(3-\gamma)}}\mathcal{P}w_{\beta_{\sharp},0}g_{\sigma}\|^{2}_{A}+\|(l^{-\mathbf{Z}}\delta x+l)^{\mathtt{q}}\nu^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{P}^{\perp}w_{\beta_{\sharp},0}g_{\sigma}\|^{2}_{A}\,.

Here, compared with Lemma
3.4
, we omit the constant factors about the parameters
\delta,\hbar
, due to the factors on the parameter
l
is dominant. The above iteration form means that once an
x
-polynomial weight
(l^{-\mathbf{Z}}\delta x+l)^{\frac{1}{3-\gamma}}
is reduced, a
|v|
-polynomial weight
(1+|v|)^{\beta_{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2}}
will be increased. In the quantity (
1.73
), we take
a=\frac{15}{8(3-\gamma)}>0
and
\mathbf{Z}=6
(it will be always taken as 6 in our proof). Then the quantity
\mathscr{E}_{2}^{A}(g_{\sigma})
can be bounded by

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{2}^{A}(g_{\sigma})\lesssim l^{-\frac{125}{24(3-\gamma)}}\big[l^{\frac{59-24\gamma}{24(3-\gamma)}}\psi_{6;\frac{15}{8(3-\gamma)},0}\big]+\cdots\lesssim l^{-\frac{3(3+\gamma)}{32(3-\gamma)}}\Xi^{A}[\tfrac{59-24\gamma}{24(3-\gamma)};\tfrac{15}{8(3-\gamma)},0](g_{\sigma})+\cdots

for
-3<\gamma\leq 1
, where we have used the relation
l^{a}\psi_{6;b,c}=\Xi^{A}[a;b,c](g_{\sigma})
defined in (
1.40
).

Together with the spatial-velocity indices iteration form (
1.74
), we will apply the so-called interleaved iteration process to dominate the quantity
\Xi^{A}[\tfrac{59-24\gamma}{24(3-\gamma)};\tfrac{15}{8(3-\gamma)},0](g_{\sigma})
. The process can be intuitively expressed by the following Figure
1
.

\mathscr{E}_{2}^{A}(g_{\sigma})

.
